Crests (`reglagesRessac`: 8 px apart at zoom 8, ×1.32 per zoom level, one every 4.5 s) roll in through `zone`; within about one spacing of the shore they turn from water-line ink into broken paper-white foam (value noise of about 7 screen px, mixed between two octaves so it is stable while zooming) and leave a foam trail that keeps retreating once the crest has "reached the beach" (the crest's position goes negative). A foam rim (`bord`, 2.5 to 9 px) always edges the shore. Fronts waver along the coast with noise in zoom-13 tile units, kept to octaves wider than 60 px on screen; the noise repeats every 256 tiles so the per-frame origin does not change it. From zoom 8 to 10 there are fewer crests and less foam (`force`). It fades in between 7.6 and 8.6 while the swell fades out.
  - Needs WebGL2, `Worker`, `OffscreenCanvas` and `createImageBitmap` (Safari 16.4+), else nothing. Not under reduced motion. Measured at CPU ÷4 in a phone context: same frame rate as without it.
  - Tests: `carte.getLayer('ressac').implementation.etat()` lists the tiles on screen and their state.
- **Ships.** These are 3D bezaisen (kitamae-bune), drawn by `fabriquerBateau()` in `modeles3d.js` and rendered by a second three.js custom layer, `bateaux-3d`, with its own renderer on the shared GL context. It uses the same mirror basis and camera-relative sun as `couche3d.js`.
  - The waterline is y = 0 and the hull dips below it. The relief's sea surface (0 m) writes depth, so it hides the hull's underside.
  - At most `MAX_BATEAUX` (2) sail at once. A new one is tried every `ENTRE_BATEAUX` seconds.
  - Each trip is a straight line toward one of `PORTS` (Edo-period ports, each with a seaward bearing). It starts at least `LARGE_DEPART` (46px at zoom 5, about 90 km) from any coast. It ends where the line comes within `LARGE_FIN` (30px) of a coast.
  - 60% of ships sail in toward the port and 40% sail out. Each grows in, rocks as it sails, and shrinks away at the end.
  - They grow when you zoom in and shrink when you zoom out, but more slowly than the map: `TAILLE_BATEAU` = 22px tall at zoom 5, ×1.5 per zoom level (`CROISSANCE_BATEAU` = 0.6, while the map doubles). Their speed is fixed in map units (`VITESSE_BATEAU` = 3px/s at zoom 5).
  - The owner asked for both. First, a constant screen size looked wrong while zooming. Then, growing exactly with the map (40px at zoom 5) made a ship as big as Sado island: they asked for ships in proportion with the pins and legends.
  - Between zoom 6.6 and 7.2 (`FONDU_BATEAUX`) the material's opacity fades them out, so a 150px ship does not pop away at `ZOOM_CALME`.
  - A trip is rejected if `queryTerrainElevation` finds land along it (an islet too small for the Natural Earth coast), if the start is not `bienVisible`, or if another ship is within 140px.
- **Creatures.** `BETES` lists a whale (real whale-watching spots) and a sea serpent (open sea). Each plays a CSS scene when `mer.js` adds `.joue` (scene lengths are in `style.css`). A spot is used only if it passes `bienVisible`: on screen, clear of the sea names, and not behind relief (`map.unproject(map.project(spot))` must land near the spot).
  - `jouerScene(nom, [lng, lat])` is exported for tests: `(await import('/mer.js')).jouerScene('baleine', [134.4, 33.05])`.
- **Typhoon (2026-10-08).** The owner asked for a storm every 15 minutes, seen only from afar, in the spirit of the sea serpent. From animated examples they chose a typhoon (the other option was Raijin, the thunder god, on a black cloud), with the first one a minute after arrival.
  - **When.** Real time (`performance.now`): `TYPHON.premier` (60 s) after the sea starts, then `TYPHON.entre` (15 min) after each start. It is checked by the sea clock, which only runs in the far view with the page visible, so a typhoon that falls due while the visitor is zoomed in comes as soon as they zoom out. Zooming in during one ends it (`finirTyphon`, from `majCalme`).
  - **Where.** `cheminTyphon` draws a path in the sea on screen: an end point at random on screen (`bienVisible`, at least `TYPHON.large` = 26px at zoom 5, about 55 km, from Japan's coast), a start 150 to 210px (zoom 5) away toward the south (bearing 150–220° from the end), so it travels north like real typhoons. The start must be within the screen + 25%, the midpoint on screen, and every point of the way at sea (the distance image, plus `queryTerrainElevation` ≤ 5 m). 40 tries, else it retries 5 s later. Fixed routes were tried first: on the phone start view (rotated, 390px wide) all seven left the screen.
  - **How it moves.** An HTML marker moved with `setLngLat` at each sea-clock tick (eased along its path, then `reperes.verifier`), inserted after `#papier`, so it passes under the pins. Its width is `TYPHON.taille` (210px at zoom 5) × the ships' growth (`CROISSANCE_BATEAU`), set as `--taille-typhon` on its own element.
  - **The drawing.** `dessinTyphon()` computes it in the browser: about 230 overlapping round puffs (the eye wall and three arms), drawn as one path each for the ink outline, the cloud and a light highlight. This keeps 35 KB of SVG out of the file. The spiral SVG sits in an HTML div that rotates (CSS, counter-clockwise like a northern-hemisphere typhoon, `will-change`) inside a div squashed to 0.58 for the pitched map. The rotation is a composited layer, so nothing is repainted. Rain, two lightning bolts and its shadow on the sea are an unrotated SVG underneath; wind lines go on top. Its 26 s life (grow, turn, fade) is the CSS `typhon-vie`: keep it equal to `TYPHON.duree`.
  - Tests: `(await import('/mer.js')).jouerTyphon()` starts one now (false if no path fits the view). Measured at CPU ÷4 in a phone context: no frame-rate change.
- **Reduced motion.** Nothing in the sea moves:
  - the swell is drawn still (its clock never starts), with the islands' shadow; no surf;
  - three.js is not loaded early and there are no ships;
  - the clock never starts and the CSS animations are off;
  - no creature appears.
- **Filming it.** For GIFs, Playwright can slow the page: wrap `requestAnimationFrame` timestamps and set `playbackRate` on `document.getAnimations()` in an init script. Then set the GIF frame times back to real speed.

### When to go: the places' seasons (`site/saisons.js`, 2026-10-12)

The owner chose, from examples, a 12-month calendar plus a "best time to visit" block on each place card (options B + C; A was four season chips in the Categories menu).
- **The data** is the Sheet's `Saisons` column (alias list in `construireLieux`), read by `lireSaisons`: items separated by `;`, each a French keyword then months 1–12 (`cerisiers 4; automne 11-12`, `hiver 12-2` wraps over the new year, `fleurs 4,5`), or `toute l'année`. Keywords (accents and case ignored, English and Japanese aliases accepted, matched on the first word so `fête` never reads as `été`): `cerisiers`, `fleurs`, `verdure`, `été`, `automne` (autumn colours, including red glasswort), `hiver` (snow, ice, drift ice, winter illuminations, clear winter Fuji), `fête` (a festival at the place). `SORTES` gives each its emoji, colour and labels (`saison_*` in `TEXTES`). A real season wins over `toute l'année`. Unknown words are ignored; an empty cell shows nothing.
- **Filling it.** On 2026-10-12 I chose the values for the 134 places (62 "all year", 37 autumn, 13 cherry, 13 summer, 11 festival, 10 flowers, 10 winter, 3 greenery) and a one-off robot function wrote them into the empty cells (the JSON and the function were removed afterwards; they are in git history). `colonnes_` adds the `Saisons` column if missing (like `Robot`). For new rows, Gemini call 2 returns `saisons` in the same format (rules in `CONSIGNES_REDIGER`), cleaned by `nettoyerSaisons_` and written only into an empty cell. After that the Sheet is the source: the JSON is not read by the site.
- **The calendar** (`#btn-saisons` "When to go", icon-only on phones; `#panneau-saisons`, opened by `ouvrirSaisons`, one of the `PANNEAUX`): `contenuPanneau` draws the 12 months (kanji month + short name, an underline in the season's colour), opening on the current month in Japan, then the places at their best that month grouped by season (each place under its first season, north to south; a tap flies to it), then "Show the n places on the map". `montrerSaison(mois)` gives the other pins `.hors-saison` (opacity 0.2, greyed) or, for places beautiful all year, `.toute-annee` (0.55), and frames the season's places. The pill `#resume-saison` (reusing the itinerary summary styles) shows the month and count; a tap reopens the calendar (changing month there moves the map too), × clears.
- **The card**: `blocFiche` fills `#fiche-saisons` under the category line: a 12-month bar coloured by season, then one line per season with its months (`texteMois` groups consecutive months: "November – December", "December – February"), or "Beautiful all year round".
- Texts: `saisons*` and `saison_*` in `TEXTES`. Tests: route the Sheet CSV in Playwright and add a `Saisons` column (the 2026-10-12 session's `test_saisons.py`).

### Time of day and seasons (`site/ambiance.js`, `site/teintes.js`, `site/phenologie.js`, `site/saisons-carte.js`, `site/particules.js`)

On 2026-10-12 the owner chose, from an examples page, a map that follows the real moment in Japan: day, sunrise or sunset, night, and the season, all automatic, with the time in Japan shown. After trying it the same day they asked for: particles only when zoomed in, more realistic falling leaves, a light coming from each place at night, a small day/night button, more seasonal details on the map (frozen lakes in winter, more flowers in spring…), 3D models that follow the season (snow on temple roofs…), and a developer-mode setting to change the season.
- **The moment.** `moment(date)` computes the sun's height at the centre of Honshū (`soleil`, approximate astronomy formulas, ~0.5°) and the date in Japan (`Intl` with `Asia/Tokyo`). Night weight `nuit` = smoothstep from −1° to −9°; twilight weight `soir` from 9° to 0° (times 1 − nuit); `matin` tells sunrise from sunset (the hour angle's sign). The season interpolates `MOIS` (per month, at the 15th: spring green, summer green, autumn rust, snow line in metres) by day.
- **The palette.** `palette(m)` returns every map colour for that moment: the sea (background, masks, lakes, rivers), water-lines, the islands' shadow, the coast ink, borders and their paper underlay, the region washes' opacity, the sky, horizon and haze, the hillshade (shadow, highlight, accent, direction: north-west by day, west at sunset, north-east at sunrise; whiter highlights under low snow) and the relief ramp. The ramp is resampled at denser `PALIERS` (same day colours; needed for the snow line) and each stop is tinted by `teinteRelief`: the season (spring/summer greens below 1,300/1,800 m, autumn rust between 120 and 2,000 m, snow above the month's snow line: 600 m in winter, 3,200 m in October so Mount Fuji gets its cap, none in July–September), then the light (golden at sunset, pink at sunrise, blue-grey at night). The sea stops keep the ±1 m margin around 0 (see Gotchas). `COULEURS` and `OMBRAGE` hold [day, sunset, sunrise, night] for everything else; the day values are the old fixed ones (`CONFIG.couleurs`).
- **At start-up** `preparerAmbiance()` (called in `app.js` before the map is created) computes the palette, and the style is built with it (`P.mer`, `P.rampe`, `P.ombre`…): no repaint of the relief at load. The style has `transition: { duration: 0 }`: with MapLibre's default 300 ms fade, terrain tiles were re-rendered once at the first frame (old colours) and never again.
- **Changes.** `brancher` (called in `demarrer`) checks the moment every minute and when the page becomes visible. The colours change only when a step changes (a tenth of `soir` or `nuit`, sunrise/sunset, or the day): `appliquer` sets ~25 paint properties at once and `setSky`. A paint change fires a style `data` event, and MapLibre then re-renders every terrain texture once (`_handleTerrainDataEvent` → `releaseAllRTT`), so a twilight costs about 20 re-renders spread over 1.5 h. Never change these colours every frame (see Living sea).
- **Outside the map style** (`teintesHorsCarte`): `TEINTES` (`teintes.js`) holds the water-line ink, foam and island-shadow colours, a light factor, the night weight (`nuit`) and the day of the year (`jour`, null for the classic map), read every frame by the swell (`mer.js`), the surf (`ressac.js`) and the three.js lights of the models (`couche3d.js`) and ships, and the sun at sunrise and sunset (`soleil`, `horizon.js`); `#papier` fades (1 by day, 0.28 at night); the browser bar (`meta theme-color`, `barre` in `COULEURS`) goes from the parchment to the night sky; the page background (`body`, seen wherever the relief is not drawn yet) takes the sea colour of the moment; `body.nuit` (night weight ≥ 0.5) makes the place pins glow like lanterns (a warm `box-shadow` on `.repere-tete`) and writes the sea, region and prefecture names in pale ink.
- **The clock** (`.heure-japon`, prepended in `.controles`, positioned above the compass): the day/night switch, the time in Japan (`heureJapon`, 24 h except in English) and the real phase (`moment_jour|lever|coucher|nuit`). Hidden in film mode (with the controls) and during the game.
- **The day/night switch** (`.jour-nuit`, `role="switch"`, texts `jourNuit`, `voirJour`, `voirNuit`): a track with a sun and a moon, and a knob on what the map shows (`data-vue`: jour, crepuscule, nuit). A tap shows the other moment: by day or at sunset, night (`force = 'nuit'`); at night, day. A second tap goes back to the real moment (with its sunset, if any). `verifier` builds `m` from the real moment `reel` with `soir: 0, nuit: 0|1` when forced; the choice clears itself when the real moment reaches it (night falls, day breaks). A forced choice gets a red ring (`.force`). Not remembered.
- **Developer mode: date and time** (`.heure-reglage` and `.reglage-ambiance`, both `.dev`): shortcuts (🌸 5 April, 🌿 15 May, 🌻 25 July, 🍁 20 November, ❄️ 5 February, 🧊 21 February with the drift ice), a date slider (day of the year) and an hour slider (quarter hours), and "Back to the real moment". They set `reglage.fixe` (debounced). Leaving developer mode calls `reel()` (`basculerDev`). Texts `reglage*`.
- **Seasonal particles** (`particules.js`, in `.saison`, inserted right after `#papier`: above the relief, under the pins): cherry petals (22 March – 6 May), fireflies (1 June – 10 July) and fireworks (`hanabi`, 11 July – 31 August, see below) on nights only, Japanese maple and ginkgo leaves (10 October – 5 December), snowflakes (6 December – 15 March); nothing in between.
  - **Only when zoomed in** (2026-10-12): `.saison.loin` below `ZOOM_PARTICULES` (7.2, back at 7.0), hidden and paused.
  - **The drawings** (redone on 2026-10-12, "not very realistic"): a seven-lobed serrated Japanese maple leaf (`erable()`, lobes computed in code: width profile, teeth toward the tip, veins to each tip, a petiole), a notched ginkgo fan, a notched cherry petal pinker at its base; each leaf or petal has its face and a paler back (`.face`, `.dos` with `backface-visibility`), with a radial or linear gradient (six autumn tints, three golds). Snowflakes are soft discs; the few nearest are bigger and paler (`.pres`; a `blur` filter cost frames).
  - **The motion**: three nested animations per element, made with `element.animate` (Web Animations) with fixed numbers: the fall (the span), the dead-leaf sway (`.balance`: it glides to one side tilted nose-down, slows and rises a little, then glides back) and a 3D tumble (`.vrille`: `rotate3d` around a random axis, `perspective` on `.balance`). Durations are random, delays negative so they are already falling. 12 leaves, 18 petals, 28 flakes. **Never put CSS variables in these `@keyframes`**: the first version did (`var(--amp)`…), and the browser recomputed them every frame: −13 % frames and slower tile loading on a simulated phone, even with the particles hidden during moves (2026-10-12, the owner felt it).
  - **Fireworks (`site/hanabi.js`, redone 2026-10-20).** The first ones were a CSS-animated SVG star that grew; the owner found it "just a shape of different colours" and asked for realistic fireworks with different kinds of bursts. `creerHanabi()` returns one `canvas.hanabi` covering `.saison`, drawn frame by frame (2D, `lighter`): a show of shells that rise from below the screen trailing sparks, burst with a short glow, and throw stars that spread with air drag (`k`), fall with gravity (`g`) and die out flickering. Kinds (`SORTES`, weighted `TIRAGE`): botan (a sphere of stars, Fibonacci directions randomly rotated, sometimes a second inner sphere of another colour), kiku (stars sowing spark tails), henka (colour change), shidare-yanagi (gold willow: low drag, strong gravity, glittering embers hanging 1–1.6 s), wa (tilted ring), senrin (stars crackling into small white bursts), yashi (palm: a few gold comets), tenmetsu (strobing stars); 12 % of the time a starmine salvo of small shells. Each star draws a glow sprite (`lueur`, one per colour) plus a streak stretched along its velocity (`trait`, 70 ms of path, ≤ 60 px, via `setTransform`); sparks get none. The canvas is cleared every frame: an earlier version kept the previous frame and faded it with `destination-out`, and 8-bit rounding left faint dark discs that never faded. 60 frames per second at most; size read only on `ResizeObserver`; at most 2,400 stars (40 % and 60 % of the stars per shell on phones and ≤ 4-core devices). `animerParticules` calls `canvas.jouer(false|true)` (moves, far view); the loop ends for good when the canvas leaves the page. Measured in a phone context at CPU ÷4, map at rest: about 300 ms of script per second (≈ 5 ms per drawn frame, so ≈ 1.3 ms on a real mid-range phone), no frame over 25 ms after the fixes (the first version had up to 32 in 12 s). Tests: `film_hanabi.py` (a 16 s screencast at night over the Sumida), `cout_hanabi.py` (old vs new cost), `arret_hanabi.py` (paused while moving and far, gone in the morning).
  - They vanish at once whenever the map moves (`.saison.repos` on every `movestart`, no fade-out, and `animerParticules` pauses every animation; also below the zoom threshold, `.loin`), and fade back in at `moveend`. Tours and orbits therefore show none. None under reduced motion, none during the game.
- **The calendar of nature** (`phenologie.js`, shared by the relief, the map details and the models): `MOIS` (moved here from `ambiance.js`), `jourDeLAn`/`dateDuJour`, `floraison(lat, lng, alt)` (cherry blossom day: ~25 March from southern Kyūshū to Tokyo, then 4.6 days per degree north, later in eastern Hokkaidō, +3 days per 100 m; 22 January in Okinawa), `picAutomne(lat, alt)` (autumn colour peak: 330 − 4.2 days per degree north of 35° − 2.8 per 100 m; none in Okinawa), `ligneNeige(j)` and `neigeAuSol(lat, alt, j)` (the month's snow line, 220 m lower per degree north), `enFleurs` and `etatNature(lat, lng, alt, j)` → [snow, autumn, leaves 0–2 (bare, young, summer), blossom]. Tuned on JMA average dates to within a few days.
- **Seasonal details painted on the map** (`saisons-carte.js`, `brancherDetailsSaison`, updated by `ambiance.js` once a day and at each colour step; `majLieux()` once the Sheet is read):
  - **Watercolour dabs** (source `saison-taches`, maxzoom 10, `buffer` 256, `tolerance` 0; layers `saison-taches-0..7` from zoom 6, before `ombrage`, so the hillshade models them). Redone on 2026-10-20: the first ones (a 32-point wash polygon per place plus flat 14-point dabs) overlapped as translucent shapes with crisp edges, and the owner saw "halos" on the ground. Each dab is now a soft brush stroke: a short 2-point `LineString` with round caps, a thick `line-width` and a `line-blur` of 40–50 % of it, so its edge fades out. Per place (`touches`): 4 + radius/3 big very soft washes (0.55–0.8 × the radius wide), six groves of tighter, brighter dabs (gaussian around each grove, bigger at the heart), and scattered dabs; opacity fades toward the edge. Properties: `c` colour, `o` opacity, `t` size class. **One layer per size class** (`TAILLES`, 12 km down to 0.45 km, biggest at the bottom, `filter` on `t`), each with a zoom-only width (`auSol`: ×2 per zoom level, ground-fixed): a single layer with a zoom-and-data width (`['*', ['get', 'w'], …]` inside the zoom interpolation) is evaluated per tile at each tile's own zoom, so in a pitched view two neighbouring tiles of different levels drew the same dab at two sizes, cut straight at their border (seen at Kyoto; a wider `buffer` did not help, a source maxzoom of 12 did). Widths use the scale at 35° N (±12 % from Okinawa to Hokkaidō). Measured (laptop context, légère, Kyoto on 20 November, zooms and pans): GPU per texture 0.70 → 0.84 ms, total GPU of the route 760 → 650 ms (fewer re-renders, see the carpet), worst frame 19–23 → 12–16 ms; 4,731 features, 0.9 MB (as before). The first version used fill patterns (little trees, flower rows): a pattern keeps its pixel size per tile level, so zooming in it spread apart, then doubled at each level; the owner found it odd.
  - **The leaf carpet** (source `saison-feuilles`, symbol layer `saison-feuilles` from zoom 10.5, before `voisins`): real fallen maple and ginkgo leaves, or cherry petals, as icons lying on the relief (`icon-pitch-alignment: map`), each at a fixed point with its own rotation and size (`tapis`: 100 + 40 × radius × strength points, three quarters in piles around seven groves). The icons come from the `atlasSol` canvas (`particules.js`) cut into `feuille-0..3` and `petale-0..3` (`addImage`, pixel ratio 4). `icon-size` grows ×1.4 per zoom level (the map ×2), so no leaf appears on the way. Only the 10 places in season nearest the centre of the view (bounds + 30 %) get one (`majTapis`, from zoom 9.5, on `moveend` and every 250 ms during a move; cached per place): the whole of Japan was tens of thousands of points, a 190 ms freeze. On 2026-10-20 the owner saw the leaves appear a second after a dive arrived (they were only set at `moveend`): now they are set while zooming in, and `preparerTapis([lng, lat])` (through `ambiance.brancher`, called by `app.js`'s `preparerVol` wrapper before every dive) sets the carpet around the arrival point (places within 25 km + their radius) before the flight; measured: leaves on screen 1.3 s before arrival (zoom 11.1) instead of 0.55 s after. Symbols are not baked into the terrain textures, but MapLibre's `Map._handleTerrainDataEvent` released the relief textures under every reloaded tile of any source: it is wrapped to ignore `saison-feuilles` (re-check on upgrade).
  - Sources: `CERISIERS` (about 60 famous spots, bloom day from `floraison`, Kawazu on 10 February), `AUTOMNE` (about 55 valleys and mountains, plus city ginkgo avenues), `CHAMPS` (lavender, sunflowers, tulips, moss phlox, nemophila, kochia, wisteria, plum, hydrangea, iris, spider lilies, cosmos, rapeseed, with their dates), and the Sheet places whose `Saisons` column says cherry, flowers or autumn this month (radius 4.5 km, they win over a list spot within 5 km). List radii are ×1.5 (×1.3 for fields) in code. Strength ramps over a few days; at night the patches dim (×0.45).
  - **Winter**: snow on the ground (`saison-neige`, the prefectures source `prefectures-neige`, filtered to snowy prefectures, opacity per prefecture code from `NEIGE_PREFECTURES` × `neigeAuSol`, the Sea of Japan side as if 300 m higher), drawn under the patches and the hillshade, colour `neigeSol` from the palette; frozen lakes (`lacs-glace` + `lacs-glace-motif`: ice colour `glace` and a pattern of cracks and drifting snow, on the `eaux` source filtered by name from `LACS_GELES`, with dates: most of Hokkaidō from late December, Suwa mid-January to February…); the Okhotsk drift ice (`banquise` + `banquise-bord`, under `couleurs-relief` so the land hides it: about 1,400 floes drawn once with a seeded random, kept by distance to `COTE_OKHOTSK` and the day's strength, 22 January → 15 February → 10 March → 1 April).
  - **Born with the map.** `styleSaison(j, p)` (called by `preparerAmbiance`) gives the sources and layers of the start day, which `app.js` puts in the initial style (`ambiance.saison.sources`, `ambiance.saison.avant(id)` before `couleurs-relief`, `ombrage`, `lacs-bord` and `voisins`). Adding them after the load re-rendered every terrain texture while the map was loading. The dabs and the carpet are filled 2 s after `load`, in idle time (`requestIdleCallback`); a `setData` only frees the textures of the tiles it touches. Layers needed later (the season changed during the visit, the developer setting) are added then (`ajouter`); layers with nothing to show are hidden (`visibility: none`). Snow on the ground only below 0.1 strength is left out (its 120 KB of outlines wait). The lakes' crack pattern is drawn the first time a lake freezes.
  - **Measuring it** (2026-10-12, the owner found the map slower): a throwaway benchmark script (2026-10-12 session) serves several versions of `site/` side by side through Playwright routes (`http://<version>.test/`, files from `git archive`), the Sheet CSV routed to a saved copy, in a phone context at CPU ÷4 with the real GPU: time until the loading screen lifts and all tiles are in, frames per second over nine `easeTo` pans at zoom 10, and the time to load tiles after each. Variants with one feature switched off tell which costs. Result after the fixes: the same as the day before the seasons (≈ 111–114 fps, same load and tile times).
- **Seasonal 3D models** (`matiereSaisons` in `modeles3d.js`, used by `couche3d.js`): `fin()` adds a `saison` attribute per vertex (kind, paint factor, random per triangle). The kind comes from the builders (`sorte`: conifers in `pinJapon`/`cedre`, tree foliage in `arbre`, lanterns in `chochin`, the kamakura glow) or from the colour (`SORTE_COULEUR`: grass and moss, maple, cherry, petal carpet, water, glass and stone-lantern windows). Each instance gets `etatSaison` (an `InstancedBufferAttribute`, written each frame for the drawn places) from `etatNature` at the place's latitude and real altitude. The vertex shader repaints: deciduous trees bare in winter, young green in spring, autumn tints; maples green until autumn, then their red; cherries pink in bloom; grass dry in winter and autumn; water frozen in deep snow; and snow on every face looking up (by the model-space normal). `actif = 0` (no `TEINTES.jour`, the classic map) keeps the original colours.
- **The light of places at night** (2026-10-12): in the models' shader, lanterns and windows glow (`vLumiere`, emissive) and a little warm light at the foot of each model (`vBas`); in `couche3d.js` (`creerLueurs`, with `matieresLueur` from `modeles3d.js`), two additive instanced meshes per drawn place: a halo facing the screen at a third of the model's height (radius 1.15 × the model's screen height) and a pool of light on the ground (a disc of radius 1.6 model units, polygon offset). Strength `TEINTES.nuit × pousse`. First tuned too strong (a white blob over the model up close), then halved.
- **Ships at night** (2026-10-12, asked by the owner): the bezaisen has two paper lanterns (stern, under the cabin's eave, and bow) and lit cabin windows (`fabriquerBateau`, `LANTERNES_BATEAU`); `mer.js` draws the ships with `matiereSaisons` (`actif` 0, `nuit` from `TEINTES`), a halo per lantern (0.3 × the ship's screen height) and a pool of light on the water under each ship (`matieresLueur`), faded with the ships.
- **For tests and filming.** `?heure=21:30`, `?date=2026-04-05` or `?mois=4` fix the moment (Japan time); `?ambiance=non` keeps the classic map (day colours, no season, no clock, no particles, no seasonal details, the models' own colours): `outils/video/tourner.py` opens the site with it, so the promo films keep their look. `?points=non` keeps every place an HTML marker, no canvas (the films animate them; see `points.js`). `(await import('/ambiance.js')).essayerMoment('2026-10-12T22:00')` switches live, as time passing would. Measured at CPU ÷4 in a phone context: the same frame rate and loading times as the map before the seasons (see Measuring it).

### Cranes (`site/oiseaux.js`)

On 2026-10-08 the owner asked for small birds flying over the map, seen only when zoomed in. From animated examples they chose red-crowned cranes (tanchō); the other options were wild geese in a V and swallows.
- **When.** From zoom `ZOOM_OISEAUX` (8.4): the first flight comes a random `PREMIER_VOL` (1.5 to 5 s) after zooming in, then one every `ENTRE_VOLS` (25 to 60 s), one at a time. A 1 s `setInterval` checks the time; the per-frame loop only runs during a flight. No flights during a dive or a game (`body.plongeon`, `body.en-jeu`) or under reduced motion. Zooming out ends the flight.
- **Tied to the map (2026-10-08).** The first version flew in screen space, and the owner found it odd that the cranes kept their course on screen while they moved the map. A flight is now one HTML marker (through `reperes`, inserted after `#papier`: above the relief and the 3D models, below the pins) whose point is the lead crane's **shadow on the ground**. `voler` picks a random side and two random heights, takes the points just off both screen edges (`surLaCarte` refuses points in the sky above the horizon), and the flight runs in a straight Mercator line between them at a fixed speed on the map (`VITESSE`, 75px/s on screen at take-off, at least 9 s). Each frame `image()` moves the marker (`setLngLat`, `reperes.verifier`), so panning, rotating and zooming carry the cranes like everything else.
- **Constant altitude (2026-10-08).** Lifted a fixed number of pixels above their terrain-following shadow, the cranes rose and dipped with every mountain; the owner found it strange. Now `altitudeDuVol` sets one altitude per flight, in real metres: the highest of 31 `queryTerrainElevation` samples along the path (divided by the exaggeration), plus a margin worth about 0.9 crane on screen above that summit (250 to 3000 m). Each frame the cranes (`.grues-air`) are placed at `transform.coordinatePoint(mercator, altitude × exaggeration, _pixelMatrix3D)`, offset from the marker's projected point (`map.project`, the shadow). These are MapLibre 6.11 internals reached through `map._camera.transform` (there is no `map.transform` in v6); without them the cranes fall back to a fixed lift of `taille × (0.45 + 0.9 sin(pitch))`. Measured over the Japanese Alps: the cranes' screen path stays within 1px of a straight line over any 1 s, while their shadow bumps by 6 to 14px. The shadows fade (to 30%) as the cranes get far above the ground.
- **Drawing a frame.** The shadows (`.grues-ombres`, `filter: brightness(0) blur`) lie at the marker point; the cranes (`.grues-air`) fly at their altitude above. Both groups turn to the flight's heading on screen, computed from the path's azimuth, the map's bearing and pitch (two projected points jittered with the relief under them). Drawn in profile, the cranes face right or left (mirrored) and lean at most 35°, so a crane flying away up the screen does not stand upright. The size is `TAILLE` (60px at zoom 10) × 1.41 per zoom level, between 38 and 120px. The fade in and out is on an inner div: MapLibre sets the marker element's own opacity. Only changed values are written (`dessin`).
- **A crane** (`GRUE`, 80 × 40, ink and watercolour like the legends) flaps with `scaleY` on `.gr-aile` (1.25 s), each one out of phase, and bobs gently. Three fly in file (`FILE`: behind and to the side, in crane widths).
- Tests: `(await import('/oiseaux.js')).faireVoler()` starts a flight now (false if one is flying). In Playwright, wrap map calls in `void …`: `page.evaluate` otherwise tries to serialise the returned map, which took 30 s.

### Music player (`site/musique.js`)

On 2026-10-08 the owner asked for Japanese music while browsing: a button just under the language switch, sound off by default, a choice of style (Japanese pop, traditional…) and mood (calm, cheerful…), play/pause, next track and volume.
- **The music.** The owner suggested AI-made songs found on YouTube. They are not free to reuse (the uploader or the AI service keeps the rights, and YouTube forbids taking the audio), so the tracks come from two Japanese composers under **CC BY 4.0** (commercial use allowed, with credit):
  - **魔王魂** (Maou Damashii, maou.audio): the whole site is CC BY 4.0, credit "音楽：魔王魂". It has 49 J-pop songs with vocals, used for the pop style. Its mp3 files answer 403 without the track page as `Referer`, so the script sends it, as the site's own download button does.
  - **PeriTune** (peritune.com): from March 2026 its new tracks have their own terms, but its "ご利用について" page keeps every track published until February 2026 under CC BY 4.0 (many old pages show no badge). The script refuses a later track. Some tracks are only in a zip.
  - DOVA-SYNDROME was ruled out: it forbids "music-centred" uses.
- **The list.** `CHOIX` in `outils/preparer_musique.py`: three styles (`trad`: koto, shamisen, shakuhachi, taiko, festival music; `pop`: Japanese songs; `cafe`: acoustic guitar and piano), three moods (`calme`, `enjouee`, `energique`), chosen from the composers' tags (バラード, 4つ打ち, ロック, お祭り, 戦闘…) and instrument lists. 41 tracks, 2 h 17, 94 MB. Café has no energetic tracks: that mood is greyed there.
- **Encoding.** Two-pass `loudnorm` to −18 LUFS (true peak −1.5 dB), so a soft koto and a rock song sound equally loud. AAC in `.m4a` with `+faststart` (plays at once), 96 kb/s for songs and 80 kb/s for instrumentals. `data/musique.json` holds per track: `id`, `style`, `ambiance`, `titre`, `auteur` (`maou` / `peritune`), `voix`, `page` (the source), `fichier`, `duree`.
- **Credits.** The panel shows the playing track's title (a link to its page), its composer and its singer, and at the bottom "Music: 魔王魂 · PeriTune · CC BY 4.0 licence" with links. The lock-screen metadata (Media Session) carries the title and composer too.
- **Off by default.** Nothing loads before a tap: the JSON on the first panel opening, a track on play. The style, mood and volume are remembered (`localStorage.musique`), never the sound itself.
- **Playing.** Each style × mood is a shuffled queue that does not repeat the last track. Choosing a style or a mood starts playing. A track fades in (0.6 s) and out on pause; changing track dips 0.25 s first. At its end the next one plays. Three load errors in a row stop it with a message. The volume slider is squared (perceived loudness); the speaker icon mutes and restores.
- **iPhone.** On iOS an audio element's `volume` is read-only (always 1). `musique.js` detects it and routes the element through Web Audio (`createMediaElementSource` → `GainNode`), created during the play tap. `play()` is called inside the tap with nothing awaited before it, which iOS requires: that is why the JSON is loaded when the panel opens, and play stays disabled until then. Web Audio is resumed when the page becomes visible again.
- **Quiet for videos.** `retrait(raison, oui)` fades the music out and pauses it while a TikTok video plays with sound, then resumes it: a `MutationObserver` on `#fiche-media` (the card's video iframe) and the tour's `sonVideo` callback (`visite.js`, when "With sound" is on). The button stays red with its bars still. A pause from the system (headphones, lock screen) shows as paused.
- **The button** (`#btn-musique`, `.musique`) sits under `.langues`; `.filtres-boutons` leaves it a 54px column, so on phones the Tour button wraps to the second row. Playing, it turns red and three bars dance (composited transforms; still under reduced motion). The panel (`#panneau-musique`) is one of the app's panels: opening it closes the others and vice versa, a tap outside or Escape closes it. Hidden in film mode, and on phones during the game and tour videos.
- **Changing the music.** Edit `CHOIX`, run the script, commit `site/musique/` and `site/data/musique.json`. Check a new PeriTune track's date. Japanese titles are shown as they are in every language.
- **Tests.** In Playwright, spy on `HTMLMediaElement.prototype.play` from an init script to reach the (non-DOM) audio element. Python's `http.server` has no byte ranges, so seeking restarts a track: to test a track's end, set `playbackRate = 16`. To mimic iOS, redefine `HTMLMediaElement.prototype.volume` as a getter returning 1.
- Texts: `musique*`, `style_*`, `ambiance_*` in `TEXTES`.

### Hidden legends (`site/legendes.js`)

On 2026-10-01 the owner asked for easter eggs: references to Japanese legends scattered over the map, in the style of the whale and the serpent. From examples they chose: legends visible only when you zoom into a region, a found counter with a "Bravo" once all are found, all four themes (yokai, heroes and warriors, gods and myths, sea legends), and about twenty of them.
- **Content.** `LEGENDES` lists 22 legends, north to south. Each has an `id`, a `region` (a `REGIONS` key from `regions.js`), `ou` (where the foot of the drawing stands) and `nom`, `lieu`, `texte` in en/fr/ja.
  - The drawings are inline SVG in `legendes-dessins.js` (64 × 60, foot at y ≈ 56). Their colours and idle motions are in `legendes.css`. They were generated once by a throwaway script and are kept as literal SVG: edit them by hand.
  - Some legends belong to a place that has its own pin (Kashima-jingū, Kibitsu-jinja, Amanoiwato-jinja). They are offset by 6 to 12 km so the pin does not cover them.
- **Loading.** `app.js` imports the module 1.5 s after the map is ready (`chargerLegendes`). If it fails, the map simply has no legends.
- **Markers.** Each legend is an HTML `Marker`: a `button.legende` with `data-legende=<id>`. It is moved in the DOM to just after `#papier`, so the place pins stay in front of it.
  - Below `ZOOM_LEGENDES` (6.5) the container gets `.sans-legendes` and the legends are hidden: you have to zoom into a region to find them.
  - Only the legends inside the view get `.anime` (their idle loop), recomputed on `moveend`. Under reduced motion nothing moves.
- **The bubble.** A tap opens a MapLibre `Popup` (`.bulle-legende`). It shows the name, a second name (Japanese, or English in the Japanese UI), the place and the story. `garderVisible()` pans the map when the bubble would slide under the top buttons, which happened on phones.
- **Found legends.** They are kept in `localStorage` (`legendesTrouvees`, this browser only).
  - The first find reveals `#btn-legendes` with its count, and each find shows a toast.
  - The button opens `#panneau-legendes`. Found legends show their drawing; a tap flies there and opens the bubble. The others show "Not found yet" and their region, as a hint.
  - When the last one is found, a "Bravo" card with a red 伝説 seal appears.
- **Sharing (`site/bravo-image.js`, 2026-10-02; per-network buttons 2026-10-04).** The bravo card (with a close ×) has "Share your victory": Instagram, TikTok, X and Facebook buttons, with one-stroke ink logos, not brand colours. Below them sits "Save the image". The legends list shows "Share my victory" at the top once all are found; it reopens the card.
  - **X and Facebook** accept only a link. It points to `site/legendes/<lang>.html`, a static `noindex` page whose Open Graph image is `img/legendes-bravo-<lang>.jpg` (1200×630). A visitor is sent straight to the map with `location.replace('../')`. The X text is `bravoPartageTexte`.
  - **Instagram and TikTok** have no web share link. On touch devices the button sends the vertical image to `navigator.share({ files })`, and the visitor picks the app. On a computer it opens instagram.com or tiktok.com/upload in a new tab, at once so the popup is not blocked, and downloads the image.
  - `imageBravo({ format })` draws on a canvas. `vertical` is 1080×1920 (made in the browser when the card opens). `paysage` is 1200×630, made once and committed: when the legends or the texts change, regenerate `img/legendes-bravo-{en,fr,ja}.jpg` by calling it from a headless page (Playwright: import `/config.js`, `/legendes.js`, `/legendes-dessins.js`, `/bravo-image.js`). Both have the `--grain` paper, the double rule, the logo, the seal, the bravo, the 22 vignettes in staggered rows, the challenge and the address. Japanese text breaks between words (`Intl.Segmenter`).
  - The vignettes are drawn from `DESSINS` as SVG images. Each one gets every non-`.anime` rule of `legendes.css` copied into a `<style>` (`.lg ` stripped). An SVG image can't see the page's CSS, and many drawing rules aren't under `.lg`.
  - The file is prepared when the card opens (`preparerImage`, `fichierPret`), because `navigator.share` must follow the tap closely. A language change resets it.
- **Tests.** `(await import('/legendes.js')).ouvrirLegende('kitsune')` opens a bubble as a tap would, and counts the legend as found.
- **Texts.** The UI strings are in `TEXTES` (`legendes*`, `legendeTrouvee`, `legendeInconnue`, `legendeAria`, `bravo*`).

### Halloween (`site/halloween.js`, 2026-10-20 → 31 October)

On 2026-10-20 the owner asked for Halloween decorations and events; from an examples page (six ideas animated on real night views of the map) they chose: a "Halloween in Japan" panel (with the yōkai hunt in it), witch hats on the pins, bats instead of cranes for the whole event, a black cat on the title scroll, a cobweb, the hitodama, the Night Parade of One Hundred Demons ("better made than images sliding around"), and the yōkai hunt. Everything in developer mode first, so they can check before it goes public; from 1 November the map is back to normal (cranes, the season's particles).
- **When.** `CONFIG.halloween = { debut, fin, public }`: active from `debut` 00:00 to `fin` 00:00 in the visitor's own time zone (a French visitor keeps Halloween evening); `public: false` → only with `body.mode-dev`. `majHalloween` (`app.js`) loads `halloween.js` when needed and calls `allumer()`/`eteindre()`: 1.7 s after the map is up, in `basculerDev`, and every minute (so it ends at midnight with the page open). Never with `?ambiance=non` (promo films). The owner checked it in developer mode and released it to everyone the same evening (`public: true`, 2026-10-20).
- **`halloween.js`** (orchestrator; its texts are in its own `TEXTES` object, not config.js: the event is temporary). `allumer()` loads `halloween.css` (a `<link>`, awaited so nothing shows unstyled), adds `body.halloween` (CSS: hats), puts the cat in `.bandeau` and the cobweb in the map container, shows `#btn-halloween` (a `PANNEAUX` entry, `ouvrirHalloween`), switches the birds to bats (`choisirOiseaux`), replaces the night particles with hitodama (`ambiance.remplacerParticules`), creates the parade and the hunt markers. `eteindre()` undoes all of it.
- **Drawings** (`halloween-dessins.js`, ink and watercolour like the legends, coloured by legendes.css `.lg …` classes plus `halloween.css` `hw-*`): `DEFILE` (side views facing right, 64 × 64, feet at y ≈ 60: chōchin-obake, a fox in kimono with a lantern pole, kasa-obake on one geta, tanuki with straw hat and sake bottle, kappa with a cucumber, oni with a kanabō, tengu, rokurokubi, bake-zōri), `YOKAI` (the hunt, front views 64 × 60 like the legends), `CHAUVE_SOURIS` (front view, wings `.cs-aile` flap), `CHAT`, `toileAraignee()` (below). The 2026-10-20 session's `atelier/planche.py` renders them large.
- **The cobweb** (`toileAraignee()`, redone the same day: the first one, a regular fan of threads from the corner, looked computer-made). A real orb web in a 300 × 300 box whose top-right corner is the map's (150 px and 18 px lower on phones: a decor behind the top shortcuts, its hub just below them; the map's UI is always above it): a hub, 19 radials to a frame polygon (`CADRE`) moored to the top and right edges or by long threads fading into the void (`LOINTAINES`, SVG gradients), one continuous capture spiral (17 turns, along each radial's fraction, so it follows the frame), the free zone and a tight irregular hub mesh, a torn hole down-left with broken threads hanging, dew drops; a fixed seed (mulberry32), so it is always the same web. Strokes keep their screen width (`vector-effect: non-scaling-stroke`). The SVG never changes; only HTML elements move, in transform/opacity (composited, no repaint): the whole web sways in the wind (rotation around the corner), a jorō-gumo spider (`ARAIGNEE`, yellow and blue-black, head down) drops on its thread (`.toile-soie`, `scaleY` on the same 16 s keyframes as the spider's `translateY`, percentages of the box), bounces and climbs back, and at night three dew glints twinkle (`.reflet`).

- **The parade** (`hyakki.js`): a layer `.defile-yokai` in the canvas container after `#papier` (under the pins). A quadratic Bézier in Mercator from one screen edge to the other through a mid point, in the lower part of the view (y 50–78 %, above the scroll), mostly over land (≥ 18 of 25 samples with elevation > 1 m), 34 px/s on screen at the start; each member (`CORTEGE`, 9) is placed every frame at its own point of the curve (`map.project`, so it follows the relief and moves with the map), mirrored when walking left, z-index by screen y (kept inside the layer by its own `z-index: 0`: without it the members walked over the legend bubbles and the place card, seen on 2026-10-21), fading in and out at the ends. Walking: legs and arms swing in opposition (`--pas` per member), body bob, tail, lantern sway, hopping for kasa and zōri (halloween.css; transform-origins inline in the SVG). Shadow (`::before`), lantern light and mist (radial gradients), foxfire (`.feu`). A tap opens a `bulle-legende` popup about the Hyakki Yagyō. Automatic: at night (`body.nuit`) from zoom 8.4, first after 5–12 s, then every 100–200 s (60–120 on 31 October), not during a dive, a game or a tour; the panel's "Summon the parade" flies to zoom 10.2 first if needed. **Lighter (2026-10-24).** While it walks, each member's SVG is redrawn (rasterised) at every frame of its steps; with their `drop-shadow` filter that cost the browser's GPU process about 28 ms of CPU per frame here (5 without the parade), more than the whole map. The filter is gone (at night, the only time the parade walks, it did not show): 13 ms. In très légère its SVG animations are paused and moved on by script 15 times a second (`gestes`, `SAUT_GESTES`), like hand-drawn animation, while the procession still glides every frame: 11 ms. It starts by itself 5–12 s after zooming in at night, so it skews measurements up close: `moyen.py --sans-defile` adds `en-visite` from the page's start, and the panel's button starts it on purpose.
- **Bats** (`oiseaux.js`): `choisirOiseaux({ svg, classe, nombre, vitesse, entre })` swaps the drawing at the next flight; a random group of 4–6 per flight, each zigzagging (`cs-zigzag-1..3`), 1.25× faster, a flight every 18–45 s; `choisirOiseaux(null)` brings the cranes back. A pale rim at night (`body.nuit`).
- **Hitodama** (`hitodama.js`, sort `hitodama` in `particules.js`): a canvas like the fireworks (30 fps, `jouer`), at most 10 blue-white flames with a tail, born near the places on screen (`lieuxDesHitodama`, temples and shrines first, recomputed at `moveend`), rising and swaying for 6–10 s. `ambiance.remplacerParticules(fn)` lets an event replace the season's particles: `fn(moment)` returns a sort or `undefined`; Halloween returns `hitodama` at night, so by day the autumn leaves still fall.
- **The hunt**: 7 yōkai (`CHASSE`, north to south) at haunted places, offset a few km where a pin stands (Osorezan, Himeji, Kyoto): yūrei (Osorezan), Oiwa's lantern (Yotsuya), Enma (Rokudō Chinnō-ji), Okiku (Himeji), hitotsume-kozō (Sakaiminato), konaki-jijii (Ōboke), ittan-momen (Kimotsuki). Markers like the legends (class `legende`, so `#carte.sans-legendes` hides them below zoom 6.5; `.anime` idle loops on screen), a bubble with the story, `localStorage.yokaiTrouves`, a toast and the counter on the button, the list in the panel (unknown ones show their region as a hint), and at 7/7 a bravo with an image (`imageBravo` now takes `sceau`, `grandes` (rows of 4 and 3) and `feuilles`; the legends' calls are unchanged) shared through the share sheet or downloaded. **Told apart from the legends** (asked by the owner the same day, so visitors know these yōkai are only for Halloween): an orange glow behind each one (violet around it at night, pulsing on screen: `.yokai-cache::before`), a lit jack-o'-lantern at its feet (`::after`, a data-URI SVG, glowing at night), and their bubbles (and the parade's) open with a pumpkin and "Halloween special" (`bandeauEvenement`, `.bulle-evenement`, an orange top rule: `.bulle-halloween`), plus a "yōkai 3/7" pill on the hunt's.
- **Measured** (phone context, CPU ÷4, night over Kyoto, map still, 12 s): median frame unchanged (5.6 ms headless), 1–4 frames over 25 ms during a parade with hitodama (0–3 without), about +150–350 ms/s of main-thread time at ÷4. The parade and bats are not in the dive MP4 (`enregistrement.js` composes only the map, names, pins and titles).
- Tests (2026-10-20 session, `scratchpad/halloween`): `essai_halloween.py [ordi|tel]` (decor, panel, parade, bats, hunt; leaving developer mode removes everything), `cout_halloween.py`, `essai_bravo.py`. In headless Edge, CSS transitions (the particles' fade-in) only start after a user input: send a wheel or a tap first.

### New places, favorites and the guided tour

The owner asked for these three on 2026-10-02 (ideas 1, 3 and 7 of a list I proposed). The guided tour is meant to help them film the map for TikTok.
- **"New" badge.** `construireLieux` gives each place a `date`, decoded from its TikTok video id: the top 32 bits are the Unix time (`BigInt(id) >> 32n`). `nouveau` is true when the video is younger than `CONFIG.joursNouveau` (7) days, so nothing is filled in the Sheet.
  - On the map: a teal "New" tag on the crest (`.repere.nouveau .repere-nouveau`). In the far view, the dot gets a fixed teal ring and a slow pulse (`pouls-nouveau`).
  - In the menu, `construireNouveautes` adds a "New places" row at the top, newest first, with dates. Category lists and the card show the tag too; the card shows "Video of <date>" for every place.
  - The tag is teal, not red: red stays for actions and the visitor's own choices.
- **Favorites (`site/favoris.js`).** A heart button on the card (`#fiche-favori`, next to share) toggles a place. The ids are kept in `localStorage` (`favoris`, this browser only).
  - Favorite crests carry a small red heart (`.repere.favori`), and a red ring in the far view.
  - The `#btn-favoris` button appears once there is one favorite. It opens `#panneau-favoris`: from 2 favorites the "Route on the map" block, then the places in travel order (each with a remove button), "Reverse the order", Google Maps routes and "Guided tour of my favorites".
  - Travel order (`ordreDeVoyage`): start from the south-westernmost place, go to the nearest each time, then untangle with 2-opt.
  - Google Maps URLs accept few waypoints (3 in mobile browsers, 9 elsewhere). `liensItineraire` therefore cuts the trip into routes of 10 places on desktop and 5 on phones, each starting where the previous one ended.
- **Route on the map (`site/itineraire.js`, 2026-10-05).** The owner asked for a trip built from the favorites: red lines on the map along the roads to take, and how long it takes.
  - **The router** is FOSSGIS's free OSRM server, `routing.openstreetmap.de/routed-{car,bike,foot}` (no key, CORS open, the same as openstreetmap.org's directions; worldwide, so Japan and its ferries are in). Its usage policy: at most one request per second, no heavy use, show the attribution and a "fix the map" link. So `demander` spaces requests by `ENTRE_DEMANDES` (1.1 s) and caches answers by URL, a trip is one request with all its stops (40 tested), and the `itineraire` source carries the attribution (OSRM · FOSSGIS + Fix the map), shown while the route is on the map.
  - One request: `steps=true&overview=false&geometries=polyline`. The line is rebuilt from the steps' geometries (decoded by `decoder`), which also tell ferries (`mode: 'ferry'`) from roads. A 13-stop trip across Japan is about 400 KB compressed.
  - **Ferry times.** OSRM gives ferries with no duration in OpenStreetMap a speed of 5 km/h (Kagoshima → Naha came out at 131 h). `duree()` recounts any ferry step slower than 6 km/h and longer than 5 km at `VITESSE_BATEAU` (30 km/h). Ferries with a timetable keep their own time (Hachijō → Aogashima 3 h, Ōma → Hakodate 1 h 30).
  - **No road.** A stop far from any road (a summit, an islet) is snapped to the nearest road: `waypoints[].distance` > 300 m draws a dotted line to the place ("+ 3 km off-road" when over 1 km). If the whole trip gets `NoRoute`, each leg is asked separately and the legs without a road are drawn straight and dotted ("by boat or plane").
  - **On the map:** layers `itineraire-fond` (a parchment casing, so the line reads on the sepia relief), `itineraire` (cartographer's red) and `itineraire-pointilles` (ferries, off-road ends, legs without a road), inserted under `modeles-3d`. `brancherItineraire(…, { id })` names the source and layers: `pres.js` has a second instance, `trajet`, for "Go there". Each leg's time is an HTML marker at the leg's halfway point (`.temps-trajet`, through `reperes.suivre`); on `moveend` the labels that overlap hide, longest legs first. Each stop's crest shows its number instead of the heart (`.repere.etape`), and the other crests fade to half (`body.avec-itineraire`). The camera frames the whole route in the space the panels leave (`cadrerItineraire`, the same padding correction as the game's `cadrer`).
  - **In the panel:** car / bike / foot (`modeItineraire`, remembered in `localStorage.modeItineraire`), the total (≈ time, distance, "including … by ferry"), and between two places each leg: time · distance · main roads (`grandesRoutes`: roads covering ≥ 15% of the leg, 3 at most; shown as expressway numbers like E2 and "Route 2" for national roads in English and French, by their Japanese names in Japanese, since OSM has only Japanese names). "Reverse the order" flips the travel order (`sensInverse`, also used by the Google Maps links).
  - When the panel is closed, `#resume-itineraire` sits next to the Favorites button: the total (a tap reopens the panel) and × to clear. On phones, drawing closes the panel so the map shows.
  - Changing the favorites, the mode or the order redraws the route (`retracerBientot`, 700 ms after the last change). The game clears it (`avant`).
  - OSRM's car speeds on Japanese expressways are about 70–80 km/h, so times run a little long (Kyoto → Miyajima: 267 km in 4 h 16). The panel says the times are estimates without breaks or traffic.
- **Share the trip (`site/partage-voyage.js`, `site/voyage-image.js`, 2026-10-12).** From a list of ideas the owner picked "share your trip", and from an examples page they chose a link plus **both** images, the visitor picking one.
  - **Where.** Once the route is drawn: "Share my trip" (red) under the total in the Favorites panel (`data-partager-voyage`), and "Share" in the summary pill (`#resume-partager`). `partagerVoyage` (app.js) lazy-loads `partage-voyage.js` and opens its window with the route's stops in route order (`it.trajets`).
  - **The link** (`lienVoyage`): `…/?voyage=<id>.<id>…&mode=bike` (place slugs joined by dots; `mode` only if not car). At start-up `lireLienVoyage` reads it and removes it from the address bar; once the places are loaded, `recevoirVoyage` sets `voyageRecu = { lieux, mode }`: unknown ids are skipped (none left: `voyageInconnu`), hidden categories are shown again, the route is drawn for that list (`listeItineraire()` returns `voyageRecu.lieux` instead of the favorites, `voyageRecu.mode` instead of `modeItineraire`) and the `#voyage-recu` banner shows the count, total and stops, with "Keep this trip" (`garderVoyage`: adds them to the favorites, takes their mode, and the route is redrawn for the favorites), "Guided tour" (`lancerVisite`) and × (`effacerItineraire`, which always ends a received trip). The visitor's own favorites are untouched until "Keep": toggling a heart does not redraw a received trip (`majFavoris`), and opening the Favorites panel ends it. The summary pill is hidden while the banner shows; `cadrerItineraire` leaves room for the banner. Hidden during a tour, the game and film mode.
  - **The window** shows both images as 9:16 previews (radio buttons), then on touch devices "Share the image" (`navigator.share` with the file and the text + link) and "Send the link" (`navigator.share` with the URL); on a computer "Save the image" (download) and "Copy link". Both images are made as soon as the window opens (`preparer`, cached per trip, mode, language and total), because a phone share must follow the tap closely.
  - **Image A "3D map"** (`imageVoyage('carte')`): a capture of the real map. `capturer` jumps the camera to the trip (`cameraForBounds`, the crop of the map container with the image's map aspect from `miseEnPageCarte`), raises `setPixelRatio` up to 2.5 so the crop is sharp, waits for the tiles (`areTilesLoaded`, at most 8 s, then 450 ms of fade), and copies the WebGL canvas inside a `render` event (before the browser clears it: no `preserveDrawingBuffer` needed). HTML markers are not in the canvas, so the stops (numbered red rounds) and the leg times (paper labels at each leg's `milieu`, longest legs first, none overlapping) are drawn on it. The camera and pixel ratio are then restored. `orientation` picks the bearing: north up unless turning (up to 40°, 0.02 per degree of penalty) makes a diagonal trip fit the frame much better, and a pitch of 50 instead of 32 when the trip stays wider than the frame (the relief in perspective fills the height). If the capture fails, the drawn map is used instead.
  - **Image B "travel journal"** (`imageVoyage('carnet')`): a drawn map (`carteDessinee`: `data/prefectures.geojson` in sand on the turquoise sea with hatching and the islands' shadow, the route in red over a paper casing, ferries and legs without a road dotted), at least ~250 km wide (a trip inside a city is shown in its region), then each stop with its TikTok cover (`photos/tiktok/<video id>.jpg`, same origin so the canvas stays readable; a local Sheet photo otherwise), its name, category · prefecture and its other name (Japanese, or English in the Japanese UI), and between stops the leg (time · distance · ferry). Up to 8 stops with photos, 10 without, then "+ n more places".
  - Both: the paper of the legends' bravo image (`bravo-image.js` helpers, now exporting `ajuster` and `dessinerLogo`), the logo, the title with its end in red (`voyageImageTitre`: `['My trip ', 'to Japan']`, the space in the first part, none in Japanese), the totals, the address on a red ribbon and the map sources (Mapterhorn · Natural Earth · © OpenStreetMap · OSRM). Two stops too close to each other (two temples in Kyoto) are pushed apart with a thin line to a red dot at the real place (`etapesNumerotees`).
  - Texts: `partagerVoyage`, `partagerCourt` and `voyage*` in `TEXTES`.
- **Guided tour (`site/visite.js`).** The `#btn-visite` panel picks places with the same region/type selects as the dice: `remplirChoix` is shared by both, and `candidats` takes an optional base list. It also offers "Only my favorites", the time per place (4, 7 or 11 s) and film mode.
  - **The tour:** an overview `fitBounds` of all its places, then each place in travel order. Each one gets a `flyTo` (zoom 12, pitch 60), a lower-third title (the Japanese name set vertically, name, category · prefecture) and a slow linear orbit (+18°) during the stay. It ends on the overview with "End of the tour".
  - **Flight duration:** it follows the distance, `min(8000, 3000 + km × 3.5)` ms. Do not use `maxDuration`: MapLibre jumps instantly when a flight would exceed it.
  - **Callbacks:** each step waits for `moveend` with a token, and calls `map.stop()` first, so the end of the interrupted orbit is not taken for an arrival.
  - **Controls:** a bar with previous, pause, next, progress and stop. Keyboard: Space, ← →, Esc (a capture listener, so the app's own Escape handler does not run). Dragging, zooming or rotating the map pauses the tour; resuming flies back to the current place.
  - **Taps:** markers and legends ignore taps during a tour, and the map's click-to-open is skipped. A tap only brings the bar back.
  - **Film mode** (`body.mode-film`) hides every button, the header, the toast and the pin labels. The bar moves to the top and fades after 2.5 s without input (`visite-calme` also hides the cursor). The attribution stays visible and open, as the OSM and Mapterhorn licences ask (see The sources).
  - It holds a screen wake lock while it runs.
  - **TikTok clips.** The owner found the tour dull with only the 3D model, and asked on 2026-10-02 for each place's TikTok to play for a few seconds. The panel has "Play each place's TikTok video" (on by default) and "With sound" (off).
    - On arrival, the place's video plays in a parchment frame (`.visite-video`): on the right on desktop, at the top on phones with the place below. It plays for the chosen time, then the tour cuts to the next place. The place's photo shows behind the player until it plays.
    - It uses the TikTok embed player (`player/v1/{id}`, `autoplay=1&muted=1`, every control off) and its postMessage API: commands `{type, value, 'x-tiktok-player': true}` (`play`, `pause`, `seekTo`, `mute`, `unMute`); events `onPlayerReady`, `onStateChange` (1 = playing, 2 = paused, 3 = buffering), `onMute`, `onPlayerError` (3002 = autoplay refused).
    - With `autoplay=0` the player sends no events and ignores `play` until clicked: always use `autoplay=1`.
    - **Skipping the hook.** Almost every video opens with a 3 to 4 s Google Earth dive toward the place; the owner asked on 2026-10-02 to start after it. The clip starts at `debut(lieu)`: the place's optional Sheet column `Début vidéo` (seconds, `lireSecondes` accepts `4`, `4,5`, `0:04`), else `CONFIG.debutVideo` (4). Measured on 6 videos: at 3 s, 2 showed the place; at 3.5 s, 5; at 4 s, all 6. Videos shorter than the start + 3 s play from 0.
    - The player stays invisible (the photo shows) until `onCurrentTime` reaches the start: a player that autoplays from 0 gets one `seekTo`. The stay starts then. If the video loops back below the start, it is sent there again.
    - The stay starts on the first `onStateChange` 1. If nothing plays within `ATTENTE_VIDEO` (6 s), the stay starts anyway on the photo; a video that starts later still gets the full time.
    - The player takes 2 to 3 s to start. So the next place's player loads during the current stay, as a hidden `reserve` iframe that gets `seekTo` (the start) + `pause` as soon as it plays, and `play` on arrival. The first place loads during the opening overview. Measured in Chrome: the video starts within 0.1 s of arrival.
    - Sound: the player always starts muted. "With sound" sends `unMute` once it plays; Chrome allows it after the Launch click. If a browser refuses (error 3002, or a pause we did not ask for), the video carries on muted.
    - Map padding: `margeLieu` puts the place beside or below the frame, reading the frame's laid-out position (it stays in the layout, invisible, between places). `survoler` computes the overview with `cameraForBounds` corrected for the current padding and flies back to zero padding. `arreter` eases the padding back to zero.
    - Pause and resume: on the place, the video and the orbit continue where they stopped. If the map was dragged during the pause, the tour flies back to the place.
    - Testing: TikTok's video CDN answers 403 to automated browsers (Playwright, even headed Chrome), so headless runs only show the photo fallback. Route `https://www.tiktok.com/player/v1/**` to a fake page that posts the events, or check real playback in Claude in Chrome. In a fresh EU profile the player shows its own cookie banner inside the frame; the frame stays clickable so a visitor can answer it once.
- **Dive (`plonger(lieu, { fin })` in `visite.js`).** The owner asked on 2026-10-02 for a way to replace the Google Earth hook at the start of their TikToks with the map itself.
  - On 2026-10-04 they explained that they film a video's hook **before** the place is in the Sheet. So the dive is no longer on the place card. It lives in a "Dive" tab of the tour panel (`#onglet-plongeon`, `#volet-plongeon`; `choisirOnglet('visite' | 'plongeon' | 'point')`). The same day the tabs and the dive became developer-mode only (see below): visitors only see the guided tour.
  - The tab takes a position: GPS coordinates, or a Google Maps link (`lirePosition`: `!3d…!4d…` is the place itself, `@lat,lng` is the view centre; short `maps.app.goo.gl` links can't be read from a page, so it asks for coordinates). It also takes an optional name, an optional Japanese name, and a type (`toutesCategories`, which includes categories that have no place yet).
  - The tab's code (`site/onglet-plongeon.js`: the Dive and Point to point tabs and the title card) is loaded only when developer mode is on (`chargerOngletPlongeon` in `app.js`, which passes it the app's tools); visitors never download it. `lancerPlongeon` builds a temporary place. It has a crest from `creerEpingle`, its 3D model through `modeles3d.provisoires([l])` (each `InstancedMesh` keeps `RESERVE` spare slots, and a mesh is added if no Sheet place uses that model) and its prefecture from `trouverPrefecture`. `fin()` removes it when the dive stops; Rejouer keeps it. The fields are remembered in `localStorage` (`plongeon`). Without a name, no lower third shows.
  - Sequence: it jumps to `vueDepart()`, then waits for `map.areTilesLoaded()` (3 s at most) and for the preloading below (`ATTENTE_PRECHARGE`, 10 s at most). It then holds the still frame for `PAUSE_PLONGEON` (1.2 s, a clean cut point). Next comes a `flyTo` of `DUREE_PLONGEON` (2.5 s to zoom 12, +250 ms per extra zoom level; the owner asked for 2 to 3 s on 2026-10-04) to the arrival zoom. `minZoom` is set to the start zoom, so the camera never climbs first. On arrival it shows the lower third and starts the orbit. The flight is described once by `volPlongeon(l, reglages)` (start camera, flyTo options and duration), for both the flight and its preloading.
  - **Settings (2026-10-05).** The owner asked for a closer arrival, only that place on the map, a real orbit at the end ("Accès direct et orbite" in Google Earth Studio) and a point-to-point mode. `plonger(l, { fin, depart, zoom, orbite })`: the tab's "Camera on arrival" select gives the zoom (`14` very close, `13` close, the default, `12` wider, the old one; past 13.4 the models stop growing and the 30 m DEM gets soft), and "Orbit on arrival" gives degrees per second (`0`, `5`, `10` default, `18`). Both are remembered with the other fields.
  - **Orbit.** The owner asked (2026-10-05) for the orbit to start right on arrival, with no pause. The flyTo ends at rest, so during its last `ELAN_ORBITE` ms (2 s, at most 80% of the flight) the camera hook of `descendre` adds a growing turn to the bearing (`vitesse × x² / 2R`): the camera lands already turning at the orbit's speed. The flyTo rewrites the requested bearing every frame, and the hook works on a copy, so the extra turn never accumulates; `enVol` stops it at `moveend`. `orbiterSansFin` then chains linear `easeTo`s of `TRANCHE_ORBITE` (90°) on `moveend`, from the real bearing, until stopped. Measured: 9.7°/s just before arrival, 9.9°/s just after (10°/s orbit). **`easeTo`/`flyTo` normalise the target bearing to the shortest path**, so a +360° `easeTo` does not turn at all: the old "360° over 140 s" orbit never moved. Each slice checks it reached its bearing; if not (the map was grabbed, Replay, Stop), the orbit stops.
  - **Only that place.** `lancerPlongeon` hides every Sheet pin (`reperes.montrer(…, false)`) and filters the models to the temporary places (`modeles3d.filtrer`); `body.plongeon` hides the legends (CSS). `fin()` restores them (`appliquerFiltres`, `filtrer(null)`).
  - **Point to point** (the "Point à point" tab: the same `#volet-plongeon` with class `.point-a-point`, showing `.seul-point` fields). `reglages.depart` is a second temporary place (`lieuProvisoire` in `onglet-plongeon.js`: name, Japanese name and type fields under a "Start" heading; the owner asked for its pin on 2026-10-05), with its own pin and 3D model (`modeles3d.provisoires` takes both, `RESERVE` = 2). The still frame is that place at the arrival zoom and pitch, with its lower third if it has a name (hidden at take-off), bearing = `azimut(depart, lieu)` so the place lies ahead, then a plain `flyTo` (no `minZoom`: it climbs and comes down with the distance) of `min(5500, 2500 + 600 × log2(1 + km/5))` ms, then the same arrival and orbit.
  - **Smoothness (2026-10-04, "it stutters, mostly toward mountains").** Three causes, found by measuring a dive onto Mount Nantai at CPU ÷4:
    - The 0.1 exaggeration steps (see Map): fixed by 0.01 steps.
    - **The camera height.** During a flyTo, MapLibre eases the centre's elevation toward the terrain under the target, re-sampled every frame. That sample changes all the time in a dive, because the exaggeration drops from ×30 to ×1.5 and sharper DEM tiles arrive. Each change re-fits the easing, and the camera bobbed above mountains. Now `descendre` sets `map.setTransformCameraUpdate` to return its own `elevation` (`hauteurDuVol(vol, altitude, h0)` → `(zoom, centre) → m`): for a dive, from the start height to the place's real altitude × `exageration(zoom)`, following the zoom; for point to point, following the progress of the centre along start → place (the zoom goes out and back in), the start's height scaled by the exaggeration of the moment. The altitudes come from the z12 DEM tile, decoded in `precharge.js` (which also reads the start's). The hook stays during the orbit and `arreter` removes it. Without an altitude (tile unreadable), MapLibre's own easing is used.
    - **Work during the flight.** About 90 DEM tiles were fetched mid-flight. `site/precharge.js` (`brancherPrecharge`, passed to visite as `preparerVol`) replays MapLibre's flyTo path on a clone of the camera, with `cameraHelper.handleFlyTo` and the van Wijk curve copied from `Camera.flyTo`. At 48 steps plus the first 90° of the orbit, it collects `map.coveringTiles` (with the camera height from `hauteur(zoom, centre)`) for both relief sources: `ombrage` (512) and `relief` (the terrain, which loads one level lower, plus `_addTerrainIdealTiles`' parents). It then fetches them, 8 at a time, so the browser cache serves them during the flight. It relies on MapLibre 6.11 internals and is wrapped in try/catch. It runs when a position is typed in the Dive tab (600 ms after the last key) and again on "Plonger" (once per flight: promises are cached). Also, three.js and the models were built when zoom first reached 7 (about 100 ms at ÷4), and their shaders compiled at their first draw (z 8.6), both mid-dive. `modeles3d.preparer()` now does both beforehand (`chauffer`: `renderer.compile` inside the custom layer's `render`, where touching GL state is allowed).
    - Result at CPU ÷4: the place's biggest screen jumps went from 2.2 to 0.35 px/ms (12–14 → 0–3 frames above 0.3), and the longest frame from 128 to 67–94 ms, with a flight 40 % shorter. The first second (zoom 4.6 → 10) is still busy: MapLibre decoding and drawing the new tiles of each level.
  - It always uses film mode, with `body.plongeon`. The bar keeps only Rejouer (red) and Arrêter, and hides after 0.9 s so the take stays clean. Space/Enter replay, Esc stops. Map gestures don't pause it.
  - On phones the lower third sits at `bottom: 22dvh`, out of the zone TikTok covers with its caption. `margePlongeon` keeps the place above it.
- **Title card to overlay (`site/affiche.js`, 2026-10-04).** The owner overlays the place's name card on their own footage in their TikToks. Below "Plonger", the Dive tab (developer mode) has "Title card for your video" (`#btn-affiche`). It draws the phone lower third (`.visite-titre` measures: vertical Japanese name in its box, name, type · prefecture) on a canvas at ×4, as a PNG with a transparent background and the drop shadow.
  - It is always in English, the language of the videos: `type.nom.en` and `PREFECTURES[p].en`. The prefecture comes from the position, if one is filled in. A name is required (`plongeonErreurNom`).
  - In the vertical name, dashes and brackets (`ー`, `「」`…) are drawn turned a quarter turn, as the browser does. The grain tile is redrawn at ×4 (it is an SVG) so it stays sharp.
  - It reuses `chargerImage`, `adresseGrain` and `lignesEquilibrees`, now exported by `bravo-image.js`.
  - On touch devices it goes to `navigator.share({ files })`, so iOS can "Save image" to Photos for CapCut. On a computer, or if sharing is refused, it downloads `<slug>.png`. The file is prepared 500 ms after each field change (`preparerAfficheBientot`), because the share must follow the tap closely.
  - The fields are remembered (`memoriserPlongeon`) on a dive or a title card.
- **The dive recorded as an MP4 (`site/enregistrement.js`, 2026-10-19).** The owner filmed dives with the phone's screen recorder, so the Replay/Stop bar showed at the start. They asked for a box to tick, then "Dive" makes a clean, good-quality MP4, with a button to stop once the camera has arrived. The Dive tab's `#plongeon-enregistrer` (remembered in `localStorage.plongeon.enregistrer`) makes `lancerPlongeon` call `preparerFilm` before creating the temporary places: it lazy-loads `enregistrement.js`, checks `codecMp4()` (WebCodecs `VideoEncoder` with H.264 High/Main at 1080 × 1920; none: a toast, and the dive plays unrecorded) and calls `enregistrer(map, …)`.
  - **The format.** Right after the first version (9:16) the owner asked for **16:9**: `#plongeon-format` (shown only when the box is ticked, remembered with the fields) offers horizontal 16:9, 1920 × 1080 (the default), or vertical 9:16, 1080 × 1920 (full-screen TikTok). `FORMATS` gives each its map box in CSS px (1280 × 720 or 432 × 768) and the pixel ratio follows (1.5 or 2.5).
  - **The frame.** `body.enregistre` + `film-horizontal|film-vertical` put `#carte` in that centred box (`--cadre-l` × `--cadre-h`; it may overflow the screen, which shows the film's own frames anyway) with `setPixelRatio(film width / box width)`; the map switches to English for the film (`changerLangue`, not saved), like the title card, while the recording screen keeps the owner's language (its own `t` reads `TEXTES` in the language of before). `estTelephone()` follows the format, not the device (vertical = phone camera and lower third, horizontal = computer ones), `vueDepart()` adds `log2(width / 390)` (phone) or `log2(width / 1400)` (computer) to the zoom, and `margePlongeon()` lowers the place a little (9:16: almost centred, the box being shorter than a phone; 16:9: top padding 10 %), so its model and pin never rise under the watermark.
  - **Time, image by image.** `visite.plonger` calls `reglages.quandPret()` just before the still frame's `PAUSE_PLONGEON` timer, and `quandArrive()` on arrival. `quandPret` installs `horlogeVirtuelle()`: `performance.now` (MapLibre's `now()` reads it on every call, so camera animations follow), `Date.now`, `requestAnimationFrame` (callbacks get the virtual time), `setTimeout`/`setInterval` (a virtual queue; 0 ms ones stay real, they only pass the hand) and every CSS/Web animation (paused, `currentTime` set from the virtual time) stop, and only move by `avancer(1000/30)`. Each film frame: advance, render, re-render until `areTilesLoaded()` (4 s real at most), then a last render whose `render` event draws the frame (the WebGL buffer is still valid there). So the film is smooth whatever the device; making it can be slower or faster than real time. `desinstaller()` restores the real clock, restarts the pending virtual timers as real ones with their remaining delay (`clearTimeout` keeps translating their ids) and plays the animations.
  - **A frame** (`composer`, on a 1080 × 1920 canvas): the map canvas; the region and prefecture names (`.nom-region`, `.nom-prefecture`: their screen box, computed font, letter-spacing, colour, effective opacity, squashed by `cos(pitch)`, with their halo), under the aged paper (`papierVieilli`: `--grain` tiled at 240 CSS px, `--taches` cover and the radial vignette of `.papier`, drawn once, at `#papier`'s opacity); the sea names; the pins (`.repere-tete`'s box: dark ring, paper border, category colour, the icon SVG as an image, the ink tip, a glow at night; a dot in the far view); the lower third (`imageAffiche`, the title card's drawing: one per name, the arrival and the point-to-point start, picked by the DOM title's text, with its opacity and slide; 22 % from the bottom in 9:16, 52 px from the bottom and ×28/22 in 16:9, like the screens they mimic); the watermark (logo + address, top centre); the sources (`© Mapterhorn · © OpenStreetMap · Natural Earth`, bottom right). Particles and sea creatures are not drawn, and hidden on screen too. `affiche.js` exports `MARGE_AFFICHE` and `ECHELLE_AFFICHE` for this.
  - **Encoding.** `VideoEncoder` H.264 (`avc1.640028` first), 16 Mb/s, a keyframe a second, `latencyMode: 'quality'`; `mp4-muxer@5.2.2` (jsDelivr ESM; deprecated for Mediabunny but stable) with `fastStart: 'in-memory'`. 60 s at most. The screen shows the film's own frames (`.enregistrement`, over everything) with "Recording · 0:07"; "Stop recording" appears on arrival. Escape (the dive's `arreter` → `fin` → `terminer`) does the same; during the film the dive's other keys are ignored.
  - **The end.** `terminer` flushes, finalises, restores the clock, the pixel ratio, the box and the language, stops the dive, then shows the video with "Save the video" (`partagerFichier`, else `telecharger`, `plongeon-<slug>.mp4`).
  - Tests: a Playwright script ticks the box, dives on Himeji Castle (and point to point from Tōdai-ji), on a computer and a phone context, waits for the stop button, stops, fetches the `<video>` blob and checks it with ffmpeg (1920 × 1080 or 1080 × 1920, H.264 High, 30 fps; 7 s ≈ 14 MB) and its frames (the 2026-10-19 session's `essai_film.py`).
- **Address watermark (`.filigrane`).** In film mode (tour and dive), the logo plus `CONFIG.adresse` (`map.randomjapanplace.com`) sit on a fresh-parchment label, top centre, below where TikTok draws its tabs. In a phone tour with videos (`body.visite-avec-video`) the label moves just under the video frame, and `margeLieu` adds its height.
- **Layout.** The button row now holds Categories, Random, Tour, Favorites and Legends. `.filtres` has no fixed width any more; its menus are 340px. On phones, Tour and Favorites show only their icon.

### Developer mode (owner only, 2026-10-04)

The owner asked for a mode holding every tool that is not (yet, or ever) for visitors, switched from the normal map in one click. It holds the Dive tab (the dive and the title card) and the unreleased game.
- **Events.** An event can be developer-only while `public` is false in its `CONFIG` entry (Halloween was, until the owner released it; see Halloween).
- **What is hidden.** Every element with class `dev` exists only in developer mode: `body:not(.mode-dev) .dev { display: none !important }` (`style.css`). The current ones are `#btn-jeu`, `#btn-defi` (the daily challenge), the tour panel's `.onglets` and `#volet-plongeon` (dive and point to point), and the card's "Visit in 3D" link (see 3D walk). To add a dev-only tool, give it this class. To release one, remove the class.
- **Who gets it.** The owner's secret link `https://map.randomjapanplace.com/?dev=<code>` opens it once in a browser (`ouvrirLienDev`).
